@@132534567 <p> 2025 felt like a year in retreat in New Zealand literature , with seven bookstores closing down ( and another five for sale at the moment with no confirmation of any takers ) , one writers residency defunded and scrambling to stay afloat ( Randell Cottage ) and another dehoused ( the Sargeson Fellowship no longer has use of its beautiful premises in Albert Park , Auckland ) , and the year ended with failure of a campaign to save the grand old loony bin where Robin Hyde wrote her most lasting work . The house was demolished . It was terribly sad in itself and served as a metaphor for the fragile shell of New Zealand literature . <p> But all these examples concerned dwellings . Inside the homes , sheds , basements and minds of the nation , writers continued to write , to create , to serve the gods of language ; and there were a lot of very good books produced in 2025 , and a lot of people paying to see authors talk about their works at literary festivals throughout the country . Tauranga @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in October . She said the whole arts sector in New Zealand faced a funding crisis that would n't go away : " We 've all got rising costs , and the pool of money is getting smaller and smaller . How we navigate that is a problem . " But the festival announced this year that it would be staged annually instead of once every two years . The supply is there to meet the demand for arts events , including writers festivals . I appeared at festivals in Auckland , Christchurch , and Queenstown this year and witnessed the sell-out events , the long signing queues , the intelligent audiences . <p> And so the edges fell apart but the centre , I think , seemed to hold . New Zealand literature is an active and passionate industry . It keeps on keeping on , and acts as opposition to stupidity as a way of life . The Government went to war in 2025 on te reo ; one of the best responses to this weird , hostile act of the Pakeha resistance was at the Kupu MaoriWriters @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exclusively in te reo . Meanwhile Landfall celebrated its 250th issue . No one seemed to notice . But what the journal lacks in attention it makes up for in literary depth , and remains indispensable to the infrastructure . <p> As for books , there were quality bestsellers that helped bookstores and publishers stay afloat , by authors ranging from Catherine Chidgey to Jacinda Ardern to Chelsea Winter . It was a golden year for commercial fiction , thanks to authors ranging from Josie Shapiro to Zoe Rankin to Jennifer Trevelyan . I made my own contribution to the trade with Polkinghorne : Inside the Trial of the Century . I had vowed in 2024 with the publication of The Survivors : True Stories of Death and Desperation that I would never write another true-crime book and promptly sat down at the end of the year to write Polkinghorne , published in July , because I obeyed the same imperative that drives all authors whether they set out to write literature or junk : I felt like it . <p> But 2025 was not a stellar year for New @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a fizzer . The best we could do this year -- The Book of Guilt , by Catherine Chidgey , named best work of fiction of the year in ReadingRoom , and the heavily favoured horse to win next year 's $65,000 fiction race at the Ockhams -- was crucified by reviewers in England , who found it too similar to Kazuo Ishiguro 's novel Never Let Me Go . From the Guardian : " The ghost of Ishiguro stalks its pages , dragging behind it the inevitable clanking comparisons and fatally undermining the integrity of the world Chidgey has so painstakingly created . " <p> As such the best New Zealand novel I read all year was published in 1976 : I found a copy of Ronald Hugh Morrieson 's posthumous novel Pallet On The Floor in an opshop for 50 cents , and was floored by its wild originality , transported to the provinces in scenes like the small tableau where a freezing worker ( " he threw the skins of the fellmongery paint-table where the skins were trimmed and sprayed with sodium sulphate " ) hosts drinks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ carriage . " It was a very old type of carriage , with seats the full length . At one end the seats had been cut away by some bush-carpenter to allow living quarters . There was a bed , table , chairs and a small kerosene cooker . Lamps hung from the ceiling . The place reeked of beer , tobacco and dried fish , presumably shark . On the floor were fish-heads and the smell of mussels . " No one writes about this kind of hardcase , rural , isolated , weird , happy , gothic New Zealand in 2025 . There is a middle-class orthodoxy to New Zealand fiction and it 's kind of suffocating . I think it will only be rescued by a Ronald Hugh Morrieson , an Alan Duff , a Becky Manawatu -- an outsider , someone in the sticks , operating nowhere near the writing programmes at Victoria 's IIML or Auckland 's MCW . <p> There are a few authors who fit that description but unfortunately most of them are n't very good . Quality writing -- not RD address @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only metric worth anyone 's attention . Morrieson wrote to his friend Maurice Shadbolt , " I have these majestic themes in my mind , you see , big things , shapes like music . " Duff had in his mind an ambition to write with the majesty and chaos of his favourite author , Hubert Selby . Right now some rough beast with good literary sense may be slouching towards the end of their New Zealand masterpiece , waiting for it to be born , maybe soon , maybe in 2026 . <p> BEST BOOK <p> The Middle of Nowhere : Stories of working in the Manapouri hydro project by Rosemary Baird ( Canterbury University Press ) . Huzzah to the author and the publisher for this wonderful oral history of the 1960s Manapouri hydro scheme . I named it best illustrated book of the year on Tuesday ; it competed with The Book of Guilt by Catherine Chidgey ( named best novel ) and the Jacinda Ardern memoir ( named best work of nonfiction ) for best book of any kind in 2025 and I think it beats @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ like a novel , with its portrait of jokers working hard and sometimes going mad under the ground ; the lead character is Tim Shadbolt , who worked at Manapouri as a university student and gave Baird a fantastic interview . What a strange , intense place it was . There were affairs in the newly built village , even a Peeping Tom . Shadbolt took up boxing and slept with the trainer 's wife . Baird sections off her interviews in themes such as work , sex , leisure , drinking , and death . You get a rich sense of the time and the place , further strengthened by attractive use of archive photos . I loved every single page . <p> BEST PUBLISHER <p> Auckland University Press . AUP ! WTF ! Generally they bumble along as a second-tier university press , beholden to boring tomes produced by academic staff , taking on poetry rejected by THWUP , and with no interest in publishing fiction ; but they were on fire all throughout 2025 , producing hit after hit , in the shape of Mark Forman 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ histories ( The Origins of an Experimental Society New Zealand , 1769 -- 1860 by Erik Olssen , Atlas of the New Zealand Wars Volume One 1834 -- 1864 by Derek Leask ) , a splendid illustrated hardback book of flowers which came with a pair of 3D glasses ( He Puawai : A natural history of New Zealand flowers ) , Craig Robertson 's dry but thorough biography of Chris Knox , two collections of poetry which I think could go all the way and see one of them claim the $10,000 poetry prize at next year 's Ockham awards ( No Good by Sophie van Waardenberg , and Terrier , Worrierby Anna Jackson ) , great critical writing by Robert Sullivan and others of Sam Duckor-Jones , Tayi Tibble , Joanna Cho , Nafanua Purcell Kersel and others in Te Whariki : Reading Ten New Poets from Aotearoa , plus worthy projects such as a te reo translation of Macbeth , a study of racism against Pacific peoples in NZ ( Oceans Between Us , edited by Sereana Naepi ) and a history of sex work ( Untold @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an immense cultural contribution , first-tier work the entire year . Please refer to the main image , a photograph I took of a sign outside a restaurant I went to on a winter 's day in that long , poignant suburb of Pakuranga , in east Auckland . What even is a " soft opening " ? Is there such a thing as its antonym , a hard opening ? Is it like a riff on soft power ? I ordered fried rice . It was pretty average . <p> BEST SENTENCE <p> Kim Knight , NZ Herald . The veteran feature content provider at the Herald was assigned to eat a school lunch when the David Seymour programme was launched in January . She went to Otahuhu College . She was served an insulated box containing recyclable aluminium containers of beef pasta bolognese in tomato sauce . It had a lot of carrot in it and the meat congealed as the temperature dropped . How to write about it , how to structure an emotional response in a sentence that arrives at the truth and will be remembered for years to come ? Most journalists do n't know how to write . Knight can write . Her sentence was better than any epigram or zinger by any author in 2025 : " I have opened a container of sadness and carrots . " <p> BEST SHORT STORY <p> " Biography " by Kelly Ana Morey . I get sent a great many short @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ very many read like photocopies of each other -- somebody moaning about something , nothing much happening , conventional English . Kelly Ana never did anything conventional . The amazing thing about " Biography " is that it had been entered into a national short story contest and got nowhere , and yet it was plainly in a different class to the stories that judges later chose as the best , a work of actual genius , a family memoir made into fiction , one of the greatest things she ever wrote . Kelly Ana died in July . Also due great praise this year were " Impact " by Rachael Taylor , about a teacher who crashes her car ; " Trina and Cat " by Breton Dukes , about two women ; two stories that featured in the Sargeson Prize , third-placed " Orange Warning " by Kate Duignan and winner of the secondary school division , " Piece of the Furniture " by Waiuku College Year 13 student Brooke Smith ; the experimental , very funny " Collective Tissue " by Craig Cliff in Landfall ; and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about an ex-athlete with two girlfriends , due to appear in the Listener Christmas issue next week . <p> BEST POEM <p> " Estinceler " by Joanna Cho . I loved the literary festival held on a blazing hot weekend in January in Hamilton , was dazzled by the huge crowds filling the Aotea Centre with excited chit and chat in Auckland , and felt the Christchurch WORD event was small but perfectly formed . And so I enjoyed the whole lot but the one I enjoyed most was at Queenstown , or more specifically at the weird , arid , strangely loveable precinct of a business park out near the airport . It made for a strangely intimate setting and you could sense a feeling of wonder that culture had arrived at Queenstown , likely the most philistine town in New Zealand . The festival was superbly programmed and my personal highlight was chairing Damien Wilkins to discuss his novel Delirious . I introduced it as the most significant New Zealand novel since Aue by Becky Manawatu and he totally confirmed it when he read out loud from a section where a couple are taken in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ body . I looked from the stage to the audience . They were in the cop car too . They were spellbound , transported to a place of grief , some in tears , some with their eyes closed , everyone silent as Wilkins 's voice grew quieter , softer , as he described the parents sitting in the corner of the back seat , staring out the window . Hell of a thing to read ; hell of a thing to listen to , and proof of the magical experiences sometimes on offer at a literary festival . <p> BEST AUDIENCE <p> Northern Region District Judges Conference , Waiheke Island . @@132554881 <p> 15 innocent people were killed when two gunmen targeted a Hanukkah celebration at Bondi Beach on Sunday . <p> One of the alleged perpetrators was shot dead by police while the other was taken into custody . <p> Naveed Akram , 24 , is facing 59 charges , including 15 counts of murder , and charges of committing a terrorist act and wounding with intent to murder . <p> OPINION <p> First , cards on the table . I write as secular , left-wing European Jew , who has lived here with his family for six years . We are a very conflicted minority within a minority . I 'm also a university lecturer who consciously avoids any professional engagement with religion or Israel . <p> Like many immigrants I prefer to keep my head down , to just try and fit in with the people and culture of my adopted country . <p> Keeping one 's head down though has become increasingly difficult . I 've been forced to confront my ethnicity and personal history a lot more since I arrived . The Hamas attack on Israel on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's murderous response , overtly or tacitly supported by Western governments , is more shocking still . <p> Like many diaspora Jews , events have led to a lot of soul searching about our history , Israel 's actions and what it means to be Jewish . Then there was Sunday 's Bondi Beach attack . It has left me and my family quite shaken . <p> A growing display of flowers and tributes is seen at a memorial outside the Bondi Pavilion as Jewish communities reflect on rising fears following violent anti-Semitic attacks . Photo / Getty Images <p> We are unnerved because we feel unsafe and unsupported here . And it 's not just about the horrendous events elsewhere . Anti-Semitism is alive and well in Aotearoa New Zealand too . <p> Saying that makes me feel awkward because anything I have suffered pales into insignificance when set against the death and destruction currently taking place in Gaza ( or historical massacres of races here and elsewhere ) . But please hear me out . <p> Under the casual category might be put people 's responses to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ vaccine mandates were akin to the Yellow Stars of David used in Nazi-era Germany . <p> My children are regularly distressed by the casual anti-Semitic comments ( with lots of likes ) that appear in their social media feeds on a daily basis . <p> But our experiences are also more institutional . One of my kids spent four years at a South Island university . On one of the student 's college committees , the treasurer was always given a kippah ( a Jewish cap ) . Guess the inference . <p> Most upsetting of all , was the short extract of Shakespeare 's Merchant of Venice play performed one night . It was a scene with Shylock , that ended with the whole cast repeatedly yelling ' the Jew , the Jew , the Jew ' . They encouraged the audience to do the same . <p> My child wandered out bewildered and in tears . When they tried to take it up with the head of the college concerned , they were politely ignored . When I took it up with the vice-chancellor , there were some @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the responsible college heads . <p> My partner has been studying te reo Maori for a couple of years . The first class she attended was going well until it came to everyone presenting their whakapapa . <p> The university 's response was not to challenge this ongoing pattern of racist harassment . Instead , it chose to investigate us . We were exonerated but no one from senior management has ever contacted us to acknowledge what we 've been through . <p> Now here 's the thing . These actions in themselves are relatively " soft " in nature . But , in allowing such behaviours to go unchallenged , as they have multiple times in history , leads to a climate in which tacit racism is normalised . <p> Tacit racism supports the more extremist ideas of far-right groups , enables racist politicians to gain power , and results in persecutions , imprisonment and massacres . <p> But there is a broader race issue that goes beyond my personal experiences of anti-Semitism here . Aotearoa New Zealand has a problem related to the fact that engagement with race @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ relations is the only discussion that seems to be on the table . <p> Multiculturalism , as a policy or political practice , does not exist in my experience . I never see it discussed in my university or talked about by politicians or commentators ( except when there is a massacre ) . But 28.8% of the population were born abroad . Asian citizens make up a similar percentage of citizens as Maori . Hindus , Muslims , Buddhists , Sikhs and Jews all have established communities here . Do none of us count ?

@@132677280 <h> From Bondi , we must learn Jewish and Palestinian safety and liberation are intertwined <p> Mourners gather after 15 people , including a 10-year-old girl , were killed in the shocking attack at Bondi . <p> Antisemitic violence is wrong in the way all forms of racist violence are wrong . <p> Events in Bondi felt close to home . The attack occurred on the first night of Chanukah , the Jewish festival of light . Fifteen people , including 10-year old Matilda , were murdered . It rightly took us back to 15 March 2019 when our Muslim whanau were targeted during their own religious ritual , in a similar act of terror that claimed 51 lives . <p> December 2025 is different to 2019 though . We live in a world fundamentally changed , and threatened , by the disregard for human life promoted through Israel 's actions in Gaza . Some argue the terror in Gaza can make Jews everywhere safer . Indeed , within hours of the news breaking , Israeli prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu publicly denounced Australian prime minister Anthony Albanese , blaming Australia @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ political opportunism before the Bondi victims had even been buried shows the lengths Israeli leaders will go to in order to create political cover for the unjustifiable . <p> Netanyahu 's response is an example of how genuine acts of antisemitism have been blamed on support for Palestine or criticism of Israeli violence . This further endangers Jewish people around the world . Antisemitism exists , and Netanyahu is happy to fan its flames if it serves him politically . <p> In response to the way Israel and its supporters weaponise antisemitism , some falsely conclude that the way to resist Israeli propaganda is by denying the existence of antisemitism altogether . Quite the opposite . At the foundations of Israel is a belief that wholesale destruction of European Jewish society during the Holocaust justifies the destruction of another society in Palestine . <p> This is why we have been working together as peers for a decade . Justine ( a Jewish New Zealander ) rejects the version of her Jewishness that is sold by Israel and Zionism . Jewish safety will never come through tanks and bombs , apartheid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ expense of the Palestinian people . Nadia ( a Palestinian New Zealander ) wants to see a Palestine where Palestinians have equal rights and the ability to raise their children safely in their homeland . She wants those committing war crimes in Gaza held to account . Jewish and Palestinian safety and liberation are intertwined . Antisemitism has been around for thousands of years . We are witnessing its resurgence due to the rise of the far right and Israel 's insistence on linking Jewish identity to Israel . Responses to the Bondi attack on social media demonstrate how modern antisemitism shows up . @@132793889 <p> President Donald Trump 's recently-issued National Security Strategy marks a decisive break in United States foreign policy . It also poses an uncomfortable challenge for New Zealand and other countries that have long depended on a rules-based order . <p> The document formalises the most significant shift in Washington 's global outlook since 1947 . It confirms , as European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen warned earlier this year , that the " West as we knew it no longer exists " . <p> By claiming that liberal democracies in the European Union were on a trajectory towards " civilisational erasure " , the Trump administration framed Europe as being in deep decline . <p> - Sponsor Promotion - <p> On that basis , it pledged to support far-right populist parties that , in its words , " oppose elite-driven , anti-democratic restrictions on core liberties in Europe , the Anglosphere and the rest of the democratic world " . <p> The security strategy also stated Europe should " take primary responsibility for its own defence " at a time when it acknowledged " many Europeans regard Russia @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it made clear the US would increasingly focus on its " Western Hemisphere " . <p> More broadly , the document prioritised state sovereignty , rejected multilateral institutions unless they directly advanced US interests , advocated limits on immigration and free trade , and outlined a vision of global order managed by a small concert of great powers : the US , China , Russia , India and Japan . <h> From strategy to reality <p> Trump 's strategy should not have come as a surprise . Rather , it formalises a set of positions and instincts that have been increasingly evident throughout the president 's second term . <p> The document was foreshadowed by the administration 's steady alignment withProject 2025 -- a suite of policy proposals advancing an ultra-conservative vision domestically and internationally . <p> It was also signalled by outlandish territorial threats directed at democracies such as Canada and Denmark by Vice-President JD Vance , who claimed in his controversial February speech in Munich that European Union states suffer from a democratic deficit . Trump has also repeatedly implied that Russia 's invasion of Ukraine should @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ contrast with New Zealand 's worldview could hardly be greater . <p> Since the end of World War II , all governments in New Zealand have firmly supported a rules-based system of international relations embodied in norms of multilateralism and institutions such as the United Nations . <p> Nevertheless , the current coalition government , like many liberal democratic states , has responded to the second Trump administration by adopting a " softly-softly " approach . <p> Such diplomacy has involved a determined effort to stay off the radar , and exercising marked restraint when the Trump administration acts in ways that show little respect for New Zealand 's national interests . <p> As a result , the New Zealand government has declined to publicly support allies and partners such as Canada , Mexico and Denmark as they face pressure from Washington . <p> It has also had little to say about the erosion of international law by the US and Israel in Gaza and beyond , refused to recognise a Palestinian state , and appeared publicly unwilling to challenge Trump 's trade protectionism . <p> The underlying assumption was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ difference on the world stage . By avoiding offending or provoking Trump , it was hoped he could be persuaded over time to soften key policies -- or at least be coaxed into negotiating exemptions from proposed tariffs on exporters such as New Zealand . <p> This strategy was flawed from the outset . It has deprived New Zealand of a clear stance on some major international issues and has risked being interpreted by Washington as supportive of Trump 's " America First " policies . <p> More importantly , quiet diplomacy did not curb Trump 's sustained assault on the multilateral , rules-based order on which New Zealand -- and most small and middle powers -- depend . <p> Nor did it prevent a 15% tariff on New Zealand exports to the US , despite US imports facing average tariffs of just 0.3% , with few restrictions . <h> Why softly-softly wo n't work <p> For countries such as New Zealand that have favoured this softly-softly stance , it is surely now time to abandon the post-war ideal of the US as champion and ally of liberal democratic states @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ shared values and interests , it has become increasingly difficult to describe New Zealand 's current relationship with Trump 's revisionist administration in those terms .

@@132856444 <p> The conflict in Gaza has been one of the defining international crises of 2025 -- with global ramifications , humanitarian tragedy , and political debate reaching deep into communities here in Aotearoa . Tens of thousands of lives were disrupted by violence , the humanitarian situation remained critical , and calls for peace and justice echoed around the world . Throughout the year this complex and heartbreaking story has dominated global headlines and sparked protests , parliamentary debate , and urgent calls for action from communities across Aotearoa . <p> In New Zealand the conflict in Gaza became more than just a foreign policy headline -- it became a moral and political conversation . Government leaders framed their positions within diplomatic practice and humanitarian support , while other voices in Parliament and the streets called for firmer action , recognition of Palestinian statehood , and an unequivocal stand for peace . <p> One of the most prominent advocates for this deeper engagement was Marama Davidson , co-leader of the Green Party of Aotearoa New Zealand . Throughout 2025 , Davidson repeatedly challenged the Government 's stance -- urging clearer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ criticism of New Zealand 's decision not yet to recognise Palestinian statehood , a decision she described in strong terms as " morally repugnant " and a stain on Aotearoa 's reputation for peace and human rights . <p> Her advocacy tapped into a broader desire among many communities in New Zealand -- Maori , Pacific , Muslim , Jewish and human rights-minded citizens -- to see international law upheld , humanitarian corridors opened , and civilian suffering addressed . Davidson reminded political leaders that words alone were not enough when communities around the world are facing loss and displacement . <p> Across cities like Auckland , tens of thousands took part in pro-Palestinian demonstrations , urging the Government to grow a spine -- to impose diplomatic and economic sanctions on Israel , support an unconditional ceasefire , oppose blockades , and increase humanitarian aid . <p> Davidson stood with these voices , arguing that justice and human rights are inseparable from New Zealand 's international identity . Her bold language and direct appeals shaped much of the year 's national narrative on the issue , even as debates continued @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ At the same time , other Maori voices called for peace and humanitarian action rooted in tikanga values like manaakitanga ( care ) and whakapapa ( interconnection ) -- emphasising that the suffering of distant communities should matter in Aotearoa just as our own experiences of colonisation and conflict matter here at home . <p> While political debate has been intense , it has also underscored the urgency of the humanitarian situation . Millions of people have faced displacement , food insecurity , and basic human needs unmet -- making the calls for ceasefire , aid , and long-term peace all the more pressing . <p> The conversation in Aotearoa in 2025 -- while at times sharply divided -- has reflected a broader global reckoning with the human cost of war , the responsibilities of governments , and the power of collective voices calling for peace . <p> In this audio interview Davidson unpacks her party 's stance on New Zealand 's response to the Gaza conflict , including what she says the Government should be doing on recognition , sanctions and moral leadership . <p> As 2025 draws to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ moment -- not just for the people directly affected , but for how Aotearoa engages with the world , embraces its values , and listens to the voices urging peace , justice and human dignity . The result is the voter will almost certainly be wrong , and also right . In 2017 , I refused to serve in the Israeli army and I spent 110 days in prison . The current ceasefire has created a rare opening , one that could become a turning point toward ending the occupation . Moments like these require immediate , strategic action . We 're at a crossroads unlike any I have seen in my lifetime , and we 're turning to you to help us seize this moment , and you can help . Support War Refusers The cycle of violence and war crimes are a self-reinforcing spiral . Violence births greater violence and so forth . When violence decreases , there 's a chance to prevent the next eruption of violence . But , paradoxically , exactly at this point is when the world turns its attention away and stops supporting the work of war resistance but that is exactly when we need to work the hardest , and plan for the long term . A paradox @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a focused end-of-year campaign to help us act decisively in this window . To do so effectively , we need to raise $50,000 . As of today , likely also due to this paradox , we have raised nearly $40,000 . <p> Without closing this gap , we will not be able to fully meet the demands of this moment , and support groups of refusers and resistance as they organize to prevent the next war , exactly at the moment when we can . We 're asking you to help us reach this goal , which you can do here . Thank you for your continued solidarity , courage , and trust .
